Pre-registered in `22dea55`, then scored once: root cause + boundary 72.4%, root cause 82.8%, replacement 0% (`bench/tmb/results-2026-10-31.md`). `bench.yml` now checks these cases too. The full set (1,145, unlabelled) could add more; a filter flags about 655.
- [ ] **DT-QA-05**: Agent-uplift eval: does an agent fix dependency breakages better with DepTime? · `P2` `L`
  - The same agent with and without DepTime's MCP tools, on the same tasks and budget; report the difference in tasks fixed. Success is the task's own tests passing, so neither DepTime nor we decide what counts as right. This answers "does it make my agent better?", which the traceback benchmarks don't. After the open DT-DATA and DT-CORE fixes.
  - **Tasks:** TimeMachine-bench's repository migrations with its own pass/fail check (47 of the 100 verified ones involve a covered package; report those and all 100, as fixed in advance); GitChameleon 2.0 (328 version-conditioned Python problems with unit tests, NeurIPS 2025; VersiCode as a backup); and fresh tasks from library releases newer than the agent model's training data, found by upgrading one dependency of real projects at a time, which also gives the exact breaking release. Old, well-known breaks (numpy 1.24's aliases) get fixed from memory, so the fresh tasks are where an uplift should show.
  - **Report:** tasks fixed, time and tokens per fix, bad fixes (pinning an old version, deleting a test), and how often the agent calls DepTime where it could help. Agent runs vary, so each task runs several times in each arm and the arms are compared task by task.
  - **How:** sealed containers with the index preloaded, run per release rather than per commit. Start with a pilot (about 20 covered TimeMachine-bench tasks × 2 arms × 3 runs) to see the effect size and the cost before a full run.
- [x] **DT-QA-06**: Investigate the unexplained bench misses · `P0` `S`
  - `never_existed` or a wrong boundary for `sqlalchemy.schema.ThreadLocalMetaData`, `langchain.server`, `litellm.llms.prompt_templates`, `instructor.core.patch.handle_response_model`, `AnthropicModel.__init__(api_key)` in pydantic-ai, and `numpy.product` (gone after 1.24.4 in the index; really 1.26). Each is an index false-absent or a wrong case: check the shipped wheels, never GitHub tags (crewai 0.67.1's tag lacks `LLM`, its wheel has it).
  - Done 2026-10-28, all six explained against the shipped wheels: `langchain.server` (lived only between the indexed 0.0.1 and 0.0.354) and pydantic-ai's `api_key` (removed in 0.0.45; 0.0.x indexed at 0.0.6 and 0.0.55) are DT-DATA-19; instructor's is an implementation import (DT-DATA-22); the other three are index bugs, now DT-DATA-23 (litellm), DT-DATA-24 (sqlalchemy) and DT-DATA-25 (numpy).
- [x] **DT-QA-07**: A fresh holdout each release, with upgrades that don't break, scored by change type and package · `P1` `M` · deps: QA-04
  - Why: every benchmark case has a real break, so nothing measures how often DepTime flags code that still works after an upgrade; S1 covers only names read absent (1.2%). And the sets fixed in advance (TimeMachine-bench, S2, the 32 cases of 2026-11-05) stop being holdouts once fixes are designed from their misses.
  - What: before each minor release, collect a new set under the DT-QA-01 rules and commit it before DepTime runs on it; score it once, then it joins `bench/cases`. Half of it doesn't break: names, arguments and imports real projects use that still work after an upgrade, taken from projects whose tests pass on both releases (TimeMachine-bench's verified repositories, and repositories that pin a covered package), asked through `check_symbols` and `diff_versions(used_symbols=...)`.
  - AC: precision, recall and false-positive rate, each with its interval, by change type (removed, moved, parameter removed or renamed, now required, deprecated, not yet added) and by package and tier; the first scoring in `bench/` and leading the README's table; a fix designed from a set's misses is scored on the next set, not this one.
  - Done 2026-11-07 (`bench/holdout/`, set `2026-11/`, pre-registered in `69db513`, scored once): a **use** is a name or call shape a real project's code has from a covered package (`uses.py`, independent of DepTime's reader), from TimeMachine-bench's 100 verified repositories (working uses only, since its breaks fed DT-QA-04) and from two repositories per package that pin an old release (`find_pinned.py`, picked by a rule fixed in advance), each asked about an upgrade to the newest release and about the same code on a release a year older. Labels come from importing each release in a sandboxed GitHub runner (`probe.py`, `holdout.yml`), never from DepTime: 303 upgrades, 3,600 uses that work and 130 that break, 1,288 excluded where the probe can't tell. Plus 35 new tracebacks from GitHub issues under the DT-QA-01 rules, 27 of their boundaries confirmed by the probe.
  - **First run** (`bench/holdout/2026-11/results-2026-11-07.md`, Wilson 95% intervals): `check_symbols` false-positive rate **3.8%** (138/3,600), precision 47.9%, recall 97.7%; `diff_versions` false-positive rate **0.3%** (7/2,399), precision 93.5%, recall 76.9% (abstains on 1,201 working uses whose `from` predates the index). `check_symbols` raises a false alarm in 16.9% of upgrades where nothing breaks, `diff_versions` in 2.7% of those it answers. `explain_error` on the 35 tracebacks: root cause + boundary **88.6%** (31/35; 74.0–95.5), root cause 91.4%, precision 94.1%, replacement 53.8%. The misses are assigned to DT-CORE-32 (138 false `missing`), DT-CORE-33 (25 breaks `diff_versions` doesn't see because a parent was removed), DT-CORE-34, DT-CORE-35, DT-DATA-52 and DT-DATA-53. Deprecation was measured, but its pre-registered truth counts docstring notes that mostly concern a deprecated parameter; the next set fixes that (DT-QA-08).
  - The 35 tracebacks joined `bench/cases` (237 real cases: root cause + boundary 97.9%, replacement 66.7%, 57.1% without curated hints); the uses stay in `bench/holdout/2026-11` with `baseline.json`, which `bench.yml` checks on every code change and index (recall may drop 2 points, the false-positive rate rise 1).
